Thank you for the lecture, Professor! As a Kazakh, I see proof of your words in the Kazakh language. When we slaughter an animal for food, we say: "Malda jazyq joq, mende azyq joq". This basically means: "The animal is without sin; I am without food" or more poetically, "You have committed no sin; my need for sustenance is the only reason". This phrase reflects the traditional Kazakh worldview of respect for nature and animals. It comes from a nomadic tradition where life was interdependent. It's a humble plea for forgiveness and a justification of the act based on the fundamental need for survival.

Anyone who has had to work a job and follow a process (or follow a process in school), while also having had a real passion at some other point in their life that they worked to develop, should be able to imagine what we lost. If societies were structured around people cultivating and sharing those passions... of course those people would be capable of things we are not capable of today.

I had an egyptian archaeologist Tell me the nile river used to go all the way down to cairo, the limestone was in a sand form and they would ship it down the nile on boats. In cairo, they would use wooden molds and fill them with this limestone sand and then fill with water. And let dry in the sun. Then they would break away the molds. It made a lot of sense to me. He also said this procedure was in some of the tombs of the archaeologist that built the pyramids. He also said even though all the archaeologists are aware of this, they don't tell anybody, because they rely so much on tourism that they like to keep the mystery.

### **@kp735** | 👍 6 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VtnRA-GNKM8&lc=UgyW16TpwMADXD4ecJJ4AaABAg)

I would absolutely recommend Prof Jiang incorporates Cahokia into his examples of ancient human civilization. It was found in central North America in the Mississippi River Valley and had enormous mounds of dirt which temples were built on top of, which shows massive levels of coordination and effort. They lived in a pretty dense urban area, and as such it is suspected that pollution from smoke, waste, and disease contributed to its collapse. Likewise, to fuel their fires and make space for growing corn, they cleared massive amounts of trees, to the point of creating a scarcity of wood. Because their diet focused almost entirely on corn, they were rather malnourished and the bits of stone that flaked off when they ground the corn into cornmeal would cause their teeth to wear down very quickly. It seems like a bad life, and there were many other nearby societies that were quite different, but they continued it for as long as they could sustain their practices. I think religion is the key as to why they stayed

For the question the prof. gave to the kid “give an example of something great we do/have now” : I think the kid had a fair point but didn’t know what to say on the spot, and I really wanted to hear how the prof. might speak on a good comparison. So I want to offer some that come to mind.
We have sky scrapers, ginormous buildings, lined with glass, with electronic elevators that can transport you from the bottom to top. I was recently in Times Square New York and we don’t just have one, but tons of sky scrapers. It’s like another world being there. Not to mention the whole sides of buildings are dynamic light shows (advertisements)
We have airplanes and jets and can transport people over oceans. We have satellites and internet that connect people all over the world. We have modern medicine that allows you to go to the emergency room for all kinds of potentially life threatening issues for which they can save you via modern technology. 
We have personal vehicles that have AC that are like a little transport pod to get you anywhere fast.
I’m sure there’s more amazing feats to mention from the modern world also. 

Now I realize that there are problems and negative side effects that could be argued for all of the points I made, but it seems to me that humans of today compared to ancient times are not less smart than previous humans. At least not inherently. We might be less developed in certain ways and have different skill sets to function and thrive in the environment we currently live in, but I think this is just different and not less intelligent. And yes the differences in the world today vs back then I’m sure has to do with our motivations as a society, as prof. points out that they are very religiously motivated where we think more individualistically of our selves and tend to be driven by personal financial gains.

### **@redcow78** | 👍 5 likes

"Our bodies evolved from apes but our minds did not" 

If you saw a small group of archaic humans, using fire, hunting together, beginning to tell stories together, make art and beginning to conceptualise their universe through religious ideas - I think it would be more easy to imagine the early steps of our minds forming. 

Maybe really what you are saying is that it's dangerous to have a materialist world view by believing in evolution. The counterargument is neither are lies or self deception. We can't lie about the world we are now in. 

The dilemma is whether we should we try to exist in this civilised world where knowledge is available, or reject it, but then also reject the human parts of us that loves what we have created and that created it with best intentions.

Ideally we would reach an equilibrium between our materialist self and our non-materialist self, far in the future. 

I agree that in the ancient Egyptians were far more advanced than we are, and long lasting. However, the Egyptians would likely also look at us, with awe. Especially our artistic achievements. This time period has been a time period of artistic and cultural brilliance that will never be surpassed, especially in things like music, film, and games, because this time period was the ultimate fusion between our non material selves and technology. What we have done in these areas are 1000 times more impressive than the pyramids, we just don't appreciate it because we are inundated by it. The issue is we are living on these pyramids now every day and every waking minute of our lives, there is no ritual or scarcity. 

However, I think now our non-material state is degrading quickly since the internet created this apex in the 2010s. The internet created a full limbic hijack, the tools available matter less now and our non material selves are being exhausted by algorithms and now AI.

And I say that cuz we have exponentially more people able to help on a project like that, across the globe. Egypt built the pyramids with much less industrialized tech to help with the sheer scale. Not to mention they did it as one singular culture present in the broader Egyptian geography at the time. They didn’t need a global system to make crazy monumental stuff. (Im sure we could do stuff at that scale with planning, but there is something to be said about their vision, relative to resource availability, societal scale, and tech progression.) Reality is crazy.

### **@jatsstar** | 👍 13 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VtnRA-GNKM8&lc=Ugwpda85dQT676FcZo14AaABAg)

As someone who has grown up in the US, but has lived multiple years in both Europe as well as China, I think the reason the students have a hard time grasping Prof Jiang’s concept of the greatness of the Pyramids vs modern day technology is because they have not yet traveled physically to see the ancient marvels with their own eyes. 

While the infrastructure in China’s mega cities (such as Beijing where they are) is often on a grand(er) scale by numerical metrics vs. their ancient counterparts, I wouldn’t call them magnificent; I would reserve that term for the Vatican, the Duomo, Notre Dame, and the Leshan Buddha etc. Seeing a giant steel and metal skyscraper tower 300meters may be awe-striking for a second, but as Jiang would say they are soulless. See a few same looking tall metal boners shooting up at the sky and quickly you are numb to them all. I still remember the day I walked through the narrow alleys of Florence only to suddenly look up when I came into an open plaza to see the magnificent Duomo in front of me. It is the beauty and intention of these structures, from the carvings and detailing, that envelope an aura of spirituality that truly makes your spirit soar (and I’m not even religious tbh). To this day I remember every notable historical relic I’ve been to, but I’ve seen and passed by hundreds of bigger skyscrapers and don’t remember a single one. That’s what the professor means when he says the building we make today “suck”.

I don't think it's as simple as to say it is in womens evolutionary favour to have sex with as many men as possible. I believe in the theory that humans are diverse like you point out right before claiming women are promiscuous evolutionarily, I believe there are different mating strategies, often called R strategy and K stradegy, basically slow and fast life history stradegy. In a chaotic environment it is beneficial to be more fast life history stradegist (because you could die any moment, so might as well spread your genes as much as possible, and have sex with many males to ensure they don't kill the rival offspring), aka live fast and die young, but in a more harsh but stable environment you benefit more from a stabler life stradegy (because in a harsh yet predictable environment you need cohesion and not to waste time competing for females, polygamy leads to enhanced male on male rivalry). Humans are individuals and some humans are more promiscuous than others, and some more monogamous. Most of us are somewhere in between. Me personally I do believe in love, and that idealistically love is between one man and one woman.

This is a fascinating theory, but it’s worth mentioning that the academic consensus doesn’t support the idea of ancient matriarchal civilizations. Archaeologists and anthropologists haven’t found solid evidence of societies where women held dominant political power over men. What we do find are matrilineal or female-centered family structures, which are different from actual matriarchy. So the theory remains speculative.

### **@Novanim** | 👍 44 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VtnRA-GNKM8&lc=Ugys6cIgb3XA4DEVOe54AaABAg)

This video marks the point where I start questioning whether this content belongs more to the realm of entertainment than genuine education. Professor Jiang is undoubtedly engaging and articulate, but increasingly his lectures seem to cross into the territory of ideological commentary — blending historical facts with personal bias and speculative claims.



Jiang’s approach often carries the tone of “everything you’ve been told is wrong. But here's the truth.” That kind of framing can be persuasive, especially to impressionable audiences, but it risks promoting intellectual hubris and conspiracies than critical thinking. It’s also worth noting that while he frequently critiques Western societies, he tends to avoid addressing the shortcomings or historical transgressions of China.



Overall it's a fun gateway to know more about history. But remember, there's a lot of lies on the internet.

### **@DJ-hy8wf** | 👍 8 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VtnRA-GNKM8&lc=UgyajeEx1hcocpF0ZYR4AaABAg)

Prof Jaing is making a misleading comparison.  He is comparing an average modern civilian to the most elite and specialized members of ancient societies, then using that cherry picked contrast to claim modern people are weaker or less capable. That is not a fair or logically valid comparison.

The Polynesians who successfully navigated the Pacific were not average villagers. They were the most highly skilled navigators in their entire culture, trained for years with knowledge passed down only to a small group. The average person in those islands would have died just as quickly as the average modern person if dropped alone into the middle of the ocean.

The same is true for things like pyramids or monumental engineering. Those feats were not done by ordinary farmers. They were designed and executed by a small class of highly trained specialists, supported by organized labor and state level logistics. The typical person in ancient Egypt was no closer to designing or building a pyramid than the typical modern person is to designing a rocket.

This argument also suffers from survivorship bias. We only remember the tiny minority of people in the ancient world who achieved extraordinary things. We do not see the overwhelming majority who had no such abilities and lived their entire lives in poverty, illness, and ignorance.

It also relies on a romanticized view of the past, where ancient cultures are treated as clever and pure while modern people are treated as soft or degenerate. That is nostalgia, not analysis.

A fair comparison would be expert versus expert, not average person versus historical outlier. If you compared modern naval engineers, survival experts, military navigators, oceanographers, and satellite based mapping teams to ancient navigators, the modern specialists would surpass them by a massive margin, both in capability and in safety.

Modern society has far more people with access to advanced cognition and specialized knowledge than any ancient culture ever did. In the ancient world, only a tiny fraction of people ever held advanced skills. Today, advanced knowledge is widespread and supported by entire systems of learning, computation, and communication.

Your argument only appears to work because you handicap modern humanity by placing the least knowledgeable modern person against the most knowledgeable ancient one. If you compare the best of modern civilization to the best of the ancient world, modern capability is overwhelmingly superior.

This is not evidence that ancient people were smarter. It is only evidence that the comparison is designed in a way that makes modern humanity look weak by stacking the deck.

> **@DJ-hy8wf** | 👍 2 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VtnRA-GNKM8&lc=UgyajeEx1hcocpF0ZYR4AaABAg.AOewyTjKDpgAOfJx0OYTi0)  
>
> ​@jaredwood1974People say we do not build things that last anymore, but that is only because they are looking at decorative monuments from the past and comparing them to disposable consumer buildings today. If you look at what we actually build for permanence, the picture is completely different.  
>   
> Modern civilization has built infrastructure designed to last far longer than most ancient monuments. The Svalbard Global Seed Vault was engineered to survive thousands of years of climate fluctuation. The Onkalo nuclear repository in Finland was designed on a one hundred thousand year timescale. Deep underground military facilities are embedded in bedrock and meant to survive direct nuclear strikes. The Large Hadron Collider sits inside a massive subterranean ring that will outlast almost every building standing on the surface. The Panama Canal expansion and major hydroelectric dams will still be standing centuries from now.  
>   
> We still build for permanence, but our priorities have changed. Ancient societies built symbolic stone monuments to glorify rulers or gods. Modern civilization builds biological vaults, energy infrastructure, planetary archives, and secured underground facilities. What lasts today is functional rather than ceremonial.  
>   
> The pyramids survived partly because they are just enormous geometric piles of stone sitting in a dry desert with almost no erosion. If those same structures were built in a wet or temperate climate they would have eroded away long ago. Many modern constructions are placed underground or in stable environments for that exact reason. They are designed for deep time in ways the ancient world could never achieve.  
>   
> And the truth is that we now have the ability to build things that could outlast every monument ever created in the ancient world by orders of magnitude. If we wanted to, we could carve information into crystalline or sapphire based storage that can endure for geological timescales and place it in deep orbit as a cultural archive that would survive for millions of years. We could create a Rosetta Stone for the entire species and house it outside the reach of weather, erosion, or conflict. Ancient civilizations were limited by stone and geography. Modern civilization can build in space, underground, and with engineered materials that do not decay, which means our ceiling for permanence is vastly higher than anything the ancient world ever had access to.

I would argue that the differentiation from hunter/gatherer to agricultural societies is more complicated than just to make offerings at temples. A lot of historians miss the fact that humans have always been cultivators of the land. We weren’t just hunters and gatherers, we were also gardeners. The idea that we were worshipping the hunting process misses the fact that we were also constantly taking into account our plant and fungus environment, memorizing which things were safe and which were not. We were not just harvesting these plants, we were taking the seeds and replanting them , we were weeding and pruning and transplanting so that plants would grow better. Regenerative agriculture is not a new idea, humans have been doing it since we became humans. I think there needs to be a greater distinction between when humans were gardeners and primarily involved in plant agriculture, vs when we transitioned into animal agriculture. I think large scale plant agriculture probably developed both for religious purposes (as Dr. Jiang suggests) but more so as humans moved from temperate into seasonal environments, and we had to stockpile food for longer periods of time. But animal agriculture I think developed primarily for religious reasons, “growing” the perfect beast in order to make sacrifices. I would love to know if other people have had similar ideas to me on this.

### **@Enkhbayar047** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VtnRA-GNKM8&lc=UgwQaqYdQA5gx2n1qmN4AaABAg)

Professor, before talking about the Great Pyramids, you have to clarify whether you believe the official dating is valid or not, and on what basis. The people who argue that the Pyramids are the remains of a much, much older civilization are, in reality, more grounded, well-educated, and well-informed than they are usually portrayed by the media and the academics in general unless you look deeper and spend some time figuring out what they are actually saying about the Pyramids. And you’ll never see them mention aliens, ever.

Many of them simply point out the difficulties, the engineering, the precise mathematical calculations and mindbogglingly the architectural works are that went into the Pyramids, along with the actual mysteries we still haven’t deciphered to this day. I can assure you, not a single serious researcher brings up the aliens trope. The idea that “aliens built the Pyramids” is basically just an internet meme born from 3:00 a.m. History Channel reruns. Immortalized by the alien meme guy.

I don't want to judge but the judgement comes naturally so I'll judge. Thirty minutes in and you mentioned aliens at least twice. I assume you're teaching to young adults but in my own experience people stopped laughing at aliens joke years ago when all a sudden aliens become more a reality than a fiction. But if that wasn't to the case, then that is quite literally uninformed and discrediting. They’re not being racist. They understand perfectly well how far advanced the Ancient Egyptian civilization was compared to the other civilizations that existed around similar time period, and respect it. But the Great Pyramids, let alone the entire Giza Plateau complex, is still more grand and mysterious than anything we’ve ever seen, ever.

In short, because you have never established that premise from the start, it came back to bite you in the back, I mean, that kid asked a real question near the end. Although I'm not sure whether he had any idea about what kind of answer to expect. But questioning alone is more than half a step so it's fine.

I see that you're rather doing a social analysis. Therefore it's not that the Pyramids were the main point, absolutely not. But it was the inspiration behind it. But facts are facts, regardless. By addressing this little tumble up, I'm sure your students wont bother you on the Pyramids analysis.

### **@arbiyanto17**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VtnRA-GNKM8&lc=UgzX63qfYiNW5ib_WP94AaABAg)

To answer the student's question at 51:23 about whether religious people today can still build tremendous structures like in the past:

I agree with Prof Jiang on how people back then were driven more by spirituality or religion than people today. I'm from Indonesia and, as a Muslim, I know very well what it is like to follow a ritual every day for God.

However, I feel the real difference is that humans today have limited their own capabilities compared to back then. In the past, people relied entirely on their internal spiritual power to achieve the impossible. Today, we have "outsourced" our brain and drive to technology.

It reminds me of what Sam Altman (OpenAI) has suggested, that AI will eventually be "smarter" than any human. We are entering an era where we put our brains into the AI, which ironically makes us feel "stupid" or less capable individually.

So to answer your question: Yes, people could be driven by religion only, but it is rare today because we have lost that raw, internal human potential. We rely on external tools (AI, capitalism, machines) to build for us, whereas the ancients used the sheer power of belief to push human capability to its absolute limit. People today would simply argue, "My God doesn't require me to build that," because we have lost the spiritual connection that made such physical devotion possible.

Modern Türkiye is slowly able to replicate the Ottoman in this regard thanks to the massive work of Erdogan. ### **@MrDavidheinemann**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VtnRA-GNKM8&lc=UgzevCFP2zSRI9X6ABF4AaABAg)

20:55  Actually, the growing interest in ancient megalithic sites like Egypt and Peru is mostly coming from people who actually interact with reality. Builders, engineers, machinists, construction workers. People who understand weight, materials, effort, and time. The ones speaking up are not academics. They're the ones who physically work with tools and machines. Meanwhile, the people with the credentials usually avoid the topic or repeat explanations that collapse under basic scrutiny.

For example, the Great Pyramid has about 2.3 million limestone and granite blocks, some weighing over 70 tons. If we tried to build it today using cranes, trucks, diamond saws, GPS, and a full modern crew working year-round, it would still take decades. Just moving the granite blocks from Aswan to Giza alone would be a massive project. Then you have to lift and place them with perfect precision. And even with all our tech, we still wouldn’t be able to replicate the internal structure with the same accuracy.

Because of all this renewed interest, people started noticing something else. They looked at the cathedrals in Europe and suddenly went, wait a second. How did they build these? Giant vaulted ceilings, stone domes, razor-straight lines, massive towers, carved detail on every inch. No steel frames, no cranes, no electricity. Where’s the evidence of how they did it? Where’s the documentation? The deeper you look, the more you realize nobody really knows.

Once you start asking these kinds of questions, you see the same pattern everywhere. The only people who seem unable to notice are the ones who spent their whole lives in school, then went straight into offices or lecture halls. People who never actually had to move stone, carve with precision, or solve real physical problems using their hands.

"1.      In seed time learn, in harvest teach, in winter enjoy.

2.      Drive your cart and your plough over the bones of the dead.

3.      The road of excess leads to the palace of wisdom.

4.      Prudence is a rich, ugly old maid courted by Incapacity.

5.      He who desires but acts not, breeds pestilence.

6.      The cut worm forgives the plough.

7.      Dip him in the river who loves water.

8.      A fool sees not the same tree that a wise man sees.

9.      He whose face gives no light, shall never become a star.

10.  Eternity is in love with the productions of time.

11.  The hours of folly are measur’d by the clock; but of wisdom, no clock can measure.

12.  No bird soars too high, if he soars with his own wings.

13.  A dead body revenges not injuries.

14.  If the fool would persist in his folly he would become wise.

15.  Shame is Pride’s cloak.

16.  Prisons are built with stones of Law, brothels with bricks of Religion. 

17.  The pride of the peacock is the glory of God.

18.  The lust of the goat is the bounty of God.

19.  The wrath of the lion is the wisdom of God.

20.  The nakedness of woman is the work of God.

21.  Excess of sorrow laughs. Excess of joy weeps.

22.  The fox condemns the trap, not himself.

23.  Joys impregnate. Sorrows bring forth.

24.  The bird a nest, the spider a web, man friendship.

25.  What is now proved was once only imagin’d.

26.  The cistern contains: the fountain overflows.

27.  One thought fills immensity.

28.  Always be ready to speak your mind, and a base man will avoid you.

29.  Everything possible to be believ’d is an image of truth.

30.  The eagle never lost so much time as when he submitted to learn of the crow.

31.  The fox provides for himself; but God provides for the lion.

32.  As the plough follows words, so God rewards prayers.

33.  The tigers of wrath are wiser than the horses of instruction.

34.  Expect poison from the standing water.

35.  You never know what is enough unless you know what is more than enough.

36.  Listen to the fool’s reproach! it is a kingly title!

37.  As the caterpillar chooses the fairest leaves to lay her eggs on, so the priest lays his curse on the fairest joys.

38.  To create a little flower is the labour of ages.

39.  As the air to a bird or the sea to a fish, so is contempt to the contemptible.

40.  The crow wish’d everything was black, the owl that everything was white.

41.  Sooner murder an infant in its cradle than nurse unacted desires.

42.  Where man is not, nature is barren."

### **@zlatinzlatev3439**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VtnRA-GNKM8&lc=UgzDd6FOeVdL7vynSQ54AaABAg)

Actually, we used star navigation and manual calculations at sea up until the 1990s. Even until 2018 was mandatory to have manual plotting as a back up. Using computers in commercial navigation is a relatively new invention. Even ARPA (Automatic Radar Plotting Aid) isn’t that old. It’s indeed impressive how the Polynesians were able to navigate and preserve such knowledge so long ago — their methods were based purely on observation and experience. But the widespread use of computers in commercial shipping only became common relatively recently. The determination of position at sea by astronomical means using only a sextant must be a skill every navigator can perform. It was used as the main method for ocean navigation until relatively recently.I'm happy that I had the opportunity to sail with navigators who practiced that in their early years.
Therefore, the comment you made to your student is not only rude but also incorrect.

I think "survival of the fittest" actually has a different meaning. Societies caring for their elderly is about the survival of the fittest, because these older people have accumulated a great deal of useful knowledge, for example, the benefits of dikes against storm surges or the dangers of avalanches in mountainous regions, both of which only occur over very long periods. Caring for the old and sick is just as important in most cultures, because if three out of ten people who have to guide a boat die, the group is no longer able to guide the boat alone and will then also perish.

### **@christravisedgar** | 👍 2 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VtnRA-GNKM8&lc=UgxlSgEGlhGlxGG-x0l4AaABAg)

Ahhh I love Professor Jiang’s work, but he was a little off on the pyramid stuff. The Egyptians at the height of their power had records dating back 10s of thousands of years, since he mentioned it, they were the ones that told Solon about Atlantis, giving him accurate dates and locations of the civilisation. We now know that the younger dryas era and the melt water pulses from the cataclysm, saw sea levels rise almost 100m globally. All major cities and towns even today are near rivers, river mouths and natural harbours. These would have all been wiped out during this time, as per the dozens of ancient flood myths that permeate the mythology of nearly every culture since.

The pyramids on the Giza plateau are the most advanced pyramids and also the earliest pyramids in the area, there is no evidence of smaller scale similar structures predating them within 1000s of kilometres. There is however pyramid structures on at least 6 continents around the world, many built on older structures, that are so far impossible to date. 

The Giza pyramids have a flowing water system beneath them, under the Giza plateau, with the quartz containing igneous rock used in the construction of various parts of the pyramids, this would allow electrical currents to be generated. There are many great researchers that have been looking at these structures as chemical processing plants or power generators. Evidence of exotic chemical byproducts have been found on the linings of the passages and chambers and recently ground penetrating radar has found massive structures penetrating deep into the ground below the pyramids. 

The jury is still out on what these structures were used for, but I’m afraid they weren’t ‘temples’ at least in any kind of way that we understand that word today!

It is a good point that the pyramids could not be made because of a lack of vision and an excess of process-oriented method. But the same point shows that the moon landings were not fake. The moon missions are an example of massive, process-oriented method. Faking the moon landings would require a capacity for holistic artistic vision that our culture did not possess at that time. The reason the moon landings look 'fake' is that they are not fake. Our artistic visions of what the moon looks like fall short of the reality, but we have preferred the visions to the reality. Also, if the moon landings were not fake, then it is not quite so true that we cannot today build a pyramid, because what was the Apollo rocket but a giant flying 'pyramid'?

### **@Chris-cf2kp** | 👍 1 likes
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VtnRA-GNKM8&lc=UgzMOfP4nIXfPzV8QCd4AaABAg)

I've been with you so far, but the pyramids are highly misunderstood and Giza is inundated by the same beurocracy you've discussed, which has pushed a lot of misinformation to maintain tourism to Egypt. They are well known to predate the Egyptians and were constructed over a much longer period than 20 years. One example citation for this is Dr. Robert Schoch, who recognized water erosion around the Sphinx which dates it to at least 12,000 years old. Giza is very enigmatic in our human history and remains elusive, but these physical properties are definitive. If anything it lends further credance to those who came much, much earlier than us in history - who we call primitive.

> **@happygolucky711** | 👍 1 likes  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VtnRA-GNKM8&lc=UgzMOfP4nIXfPzV8QCd4AaABAg.AU_TdG2N7XAAUfgP1r6-ay)  
>
> What makes a desert? I bet The area was lush and green, and forested. Then deforested and decimated in the construction of the pyramids. Oooops. Now we have no soil.  

> **@Chris-cf2kp**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VtnRA-GNKM8&lc=UgzMOfP4nIXfPzV8QCd4AaABAg.AU_TdG2N7XAAUfiw5US38I)  
>
> ​​@happygolucky711the entire Sahara desert and the northern part of Africa was indeed once lush and green. I don't say that speculatively, it's understood to have been the case. There were even water channels flowing through it at one point. There's only speculation as to why this changed, including the Younger Dryas hypothesis. Interestingly, if you look on google earth at Africa today, you can see remnants of a massive prior water flow that swept across it from the north that travelled down to the south west, indicating a sudden event that may have been geological or magnetic to cause it - which seems to have had a vast impact on the entire region. This may also coincide with the submergence of Doggerland, south of the UK. The environmental changes in that region, including Egypt, were not because of human interferance. If that was all - then there would still be fertile soil despite missing arborial ecology near Egypt. Instead, there is a vast completely dry desert, which logically seems to correlate more with climate changes. My speculative belief is that Africa once looked like South America and had its own rain forest. But that's just a, game theory

Hard disagree on the pyramids. The reason people think a prior civilization build thr pyramids is because the water erosion on the pyramids and the sphinx (for example) would date them over 10,000 years older than we're taught. Not only that, but the architecture of the pyramids seems to be of higher quality than the art that was later carved into it. This information has been misconstrued over the years purposefully to paint archeologists that go against the grain as "conspiracy theorists" with no merit. Theyre viewed as disrespectful to our ancestors, but if anything they're being *more* respectful by saying not only were humans capable of building the pyramids, but it happened 10-15000 years *before* we previously thought.

### **@MrTuttiFrutti**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VtnRA-GNKM8&lc=Ugw3gK7192huBU3fG0t4AaABAg)

Well, I don't entirely agree with the idea that we don't build, or can't build, great things today.

The difference, and I suppose what Professor Jiang was trying to explain, is that we wouldn't build the pyramids in our time simply because they wouldn't make economic sense. They would cost an astronomical amount of money, and there would be no real way to make a profit from them. It's not that we are technically incapable of building them.

On the other hand, nowadays we build major megastructures that are both impressive and useful, and I'm not referring to skyscrapers. I'm referring to infrastructure such as dams, bridges, tunnels, underground facilities, nuclear power plants, electric grids, internet networks, rockets, space stations and so on.

It is true that we do not build for spiritual reasons per se, but we cannot ignore our modern capabilities and technological progress. 

In my opinion the real problem lies in our political, economic, and value systems. I don't believe the solution is to live as our ancestors did, since we are constantly evolving, but we can and should reorient our society to live in greater harmony with the environment and with each other.

### **@loveandpeace2765**

The idea that questioning whether Egyptians built the pyramids—while accepting that humans built cathedrals—is racist, is incorrect. It actually shows that the professor has not done their homework to study the real reasons why people hold these views. The difference between the pyramids and cathedrals is vast; it is like comparing a horse-drawn carriage to an F-35 fighter jet (pyramids).

First, the pyramids are officially dated to around 3500 BC, while cathedrals appear around 900 AD. These are completely different time periods with vastly different technologies available. Second, as you noted, even with today's technology, we cannot replicate the pyramids, yet we can build cathedrals. The skepticism is not only a failure of our imagination, but a matter of pure technological capability. Cathedrals are built with small stone blocks, but Pyramids are done with very heavy regular stone blocks. 

Third, the pyramids are neither tombs nor traditional temples, though "temple" is slightly more accurate. They are spiritual and energy machines. They produce high-vibratory electromagnetic frequencies focused in the chamber. They are designed to extract energy from the ether, focus it, and release the excess into the atmosphere at a specific frequency that can be tapped by resonant devices elsewhere.

Egypt is full of evidence that technology was superior in the past. The way hard stone was worked with precision, and the sheer weight of the stones moved, cannot be handled by modern machinery. Egypt possessed knowledge of the nature of reality following the Deluge 12,800 years ago. This deluge was a planetary reset caused by the Orion Wars and the destruction of a planet called Tiamat in our solar system (where the asteroid belt is now). This happened because extraterrestrials are a reality. The Federation and Orion races were at war, which explains why the current elite is so malevolent and obsessed with control, deploying plans that span centuries.

There is substantial proof that must be considered, such as historians describing flying objects, or papyri stating there was a time when "gods" lived for thousands of years, followed by a post-deluge era where humans only lived for 100 years. We have seen both cosmic humans, called Lyrians, and many other non-human races. Humans were actually created from these Lyrians by other species to limit their consciousness and lifespan. As you can deduce, the races that did this were not positive.

There is much more evidence than this. Therefore, saying the explanation is "racist" is not only simplistic, but it is also insulting to those who study the topic. It ultimately highlights a lack of knowledge on the subject.

### **@dimitristsakatsonis5116**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VtnRA-GNKM8&lc=UgyPuTWiqm-UDuBOlYN4AaABAg)

I find your videos interesting, but I have a serious concern about the way many of these topics are presented.
A lot of what you discuss seems very close to conspiracy narratives. This is a significant issue because conspiracy theories have little to do with critical thinking, scientific analysis, or even rigorous philosophical reasoning. They often rely on suggestive connections and persuasive storytelling rather than systematic examination.
In many videos it feels like the primary goal is simply to publish your worldview and speak your thoughts, rather than to investigate issues thoroughly. Critical thinking requires deeper analysis, engagement with opposing views, and careful methodological grounding.
For example, you mention thinkers like Kant, Hegel, or Dante, but the way their ideas are presented feels extremely narrow and simplified. Anyone can summarize an argument they found online, but concepts like Kant’s idea of “things in themselves” are part of vast philosophical systems that scholars spend entire careers studying. Presenting them in a few sentences without that context can easily lead to misunderstandings.
I also notice that when your students ask questions, the responses often seem to confirm your own perspective rather than critically examining what they are proposing. If your audience truly includes high school students, I think it is also questionable to focus heavily on topics like secret societies or the Epstein case before they have developed the intellectual tools to critically understand such complex and controversial issues.
You frequently mention game theory as well, but applying game theory usually involves formal models and clearly defined mechanisms. Simply describing a scenario in a few sentences is not really the same as applying the discipline.
Your ideas are certainly intriguing, and given the current state of the world it is understandable why people are drawn to big explanatory theories. Maybe some of your intuitions even point toward real problems. But addressing such complex issues requires more than curiosity and broad interests; it requires rigorous methods and careful scholarship.
For me, these videos are interesting stories, but they should be approached cautiously. They are thought-provoking, but they do not come across as scientific or philosophically rigorous analysis.

> **@dimitristsakatsonis5116**  
> [View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VtnRA-GNKM8&lc=UgyPuTWiqm-UDuBOlYN4AaABAg.AULyVe9_m6UAUQb7GlR024)  
>
> Yet again your videos propose a very virtuous and just point of view for society. I sometimes think that perhaps the reason I struggle to fully agree with you is that I have lived my entire life inside the very system we are discussing. It is difficult not to feel deeply rooted in it, almost hooked into its assumptions and habits. Because of that, I find myself very critical when I listen to your arguments.  
>   
> At the same time, the subjects you raise carry a great amount of controversy. Forming a clear opinion becomes difficult. In many cases it almost turns into a matter of belief—whether one chooses to accept the framework you present or to disregard it.  
>   
> Recently I began reading Hegel and tried to understand the Phenomenology and the structure of his system. One thing that became clearer to me is why logic is so essential. If something truly follows from logic, then in principle there should be no disagreement about it. Mathematics shows us this clearly. Sometimes I wonder whether humanity turned so strongly toward mathematics precisely because disagreement in personal beliefs and opinions became so overwhelming.  
>   
> But even then, belief and the metaphysical dimension of the world cannot simply be dismissed. There are questions we cannot yet answer and ideas we struggle to communicate clearly, yet they still shape how we understand reality.  
>   
> I am writing this in continuation of my previous comment because I should also say that some of the ideas you present resonate deeply with my own thoughts about what a truly just society could look like. In that sense, you are undoubtedly a thinker.  
>   
> I will keep my objection regarding what appears to be a non-scientific method in parts of your analysis. But at the same time, many of the messages you are sending out to the world feel genuinely important and worth reflecting on.

calling skepticism about the 20-year Egyptian pyramid timeline “racist” is intellectually dishonest

Labeling this skepticism as racist is a rhetorical deflection, not an argument. The question concerns engineering feasibility, logistics, and archaeological evidence, not ethnicity or moral worth. The claim in question—that the Old Kingdom Egyptians could not have quarried, transported, and set 2.3 million multi-ton blocks within two decades using only copper chisels, wooden sledges, and manpower—is a technical one. It can be tested using math, materials science, and experimental archaeology.

Invoking racism to silence that inquiry:

1. Conflates critique of a hypothesis with prejudice against a people.
Questioning whether the dynastic Egyptians built the pyramids with the tools described is not denying their intelligence or humanity; it’s questioning the sufficiency of the proposed method.


2. Short-circuits empirical debate.
Instead of confronting measurable parameters—block-placement rate, quarry output, logistics capacity—the accusation shifts focus to moral character. This replaces scientific falsifiability with ideological intimidation.


3. Ignores that ancient achievement skepticism applies universally.
Similar scrutiny is directed at Roman concrete chemistry, Mayan astronomy, and Incan masonry. None of those discussions are racial in nature; they’re technological.


4. Misuses the concept of racism as a shield for dogma.
Genuine anti-racism protects people, not ideas. Science advances by subjecting all claims—especially long-standing ones—to evidence-based challenge. Declaring inquiry itself taboo is anti-intellectual.




---

In short:
Calling the question racist is an emotional veto to avoid addressing quantitative inconsistencies in the mainstream timeline. It’s not a defense of historical truth—it’s a refusal to do the math.

### **@mountainsmelodies4096**
[View on YouTube](https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=VtnRA-GNKM8&lc=UgwBoAiAL58XM_IyTxt4AaABAg)

There is not a snowball's chance in Hell that the Great Pyramid was built using Bronze Age technology.  You can't even cut the hard stones which were used for the pyramid with bronze saws.  You COULD conceivably cut a block using bronze saws, combined with water and sand grit, but it would take many days just to cut one block and the edges wouldn't look clean.  The Great Pyramid is built from millions (I believe) of these blocks and they are cut so precisely that you can't even slip the width of a sheet of paper between them.

Then there's the gematria that's encoded into the Great Pyramid in a number of different ways, showing knowledge of physics and the Universe that should not be accessible to Bronze Age humans.  Graham Hancock's book: "Fingerprints of the Gods" as well as some of his others, go into detail on these findings.  Another good reference is the Youtube channel: "Bright Insight." 

I don't claim to know what the truth is concerning the pyramids, but I can say with 100% confidence that Bronze Age technology wasn't responsible for building them.
